10 car sales flops that aged well, from the DeLorean to the Pontiac G8 GXP

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

These car sales flops, from the DeLorean to the Pontiac G8 GXP, struggled in showrooms but have since earned respect from car enthusiasts

A car can fail in the showroom and still earn respect years later. The 10 vehicles here all struggled to find buyers when new. Each has since built a following among enthusiasts. The list runs from a stainless-steel car built in Northern Ireland to a pair of American V-8 wagons, with stops at Subaru, BMW, Isuzu, Mazda, Pontiac, Acura and Volkswagen.

These car sales flops share a handful of causes. Price is a common thread. The Subaru SVX cost a lot for a Subaru. The Isuzu Vehicross, a compact two-door SUV, was pricey and tight inside. Packaging is another factor. The BMW Z3 M Coupe, nicknamed the Clown Shoe, had a shooting-brake-style two-seater shape that narrowed its audience. Shoppers at that price could choose the more conventional M3 instead.

Economic timing hurt two of these cars. The DeLorean arrived with a high price during the early-1980s recession. It also suffered from serious build-quality problems. Roughly 9,000 were built from 1981 to 1983 before the company collapsed. The Pontiac G8 GXP faced a different crisis. It launched during the 2008 financial crisis as General Motors $GM struggled. It was built only for the 2009 model year, and about 1,800 were made before the Pontiac brand ended.

Low production runs are common on the list. Fewer than 6,000 Vehicross models were made between the SUV's 1997 Japanese debut and the end of production in 2001. Cadillac built fewer than 1,800 CTS-V Wagons over a four-year run, each with a 556-hp supercharged V-8. Dodge ended the entire Magnum line in 2008. That decision also ended the 425-hp SRT-8 wagon.

Some now draw fresh attention from buyers. Low-mileage G8 GXPs often sell at or above their original sticker price, especially those with the manual transmission. The DeLorean, made famous by the 1985 film Back to the Future, has found a second life in the restomod scene. The Subaru SVX, styled by Giorgetto Giugiaro and sold for a single generation, has bodywork and door glass that now look bold.

Each slide that follows covers one car: what it was, why buyers passed on it and what has kept it in conversation since. Alongside the cars above, the list includes the Mazdaspeed 6, the Acura NSX (NC1) and the Volkswagen Phaeton. The cars are not ranked by how badly they sold.

The Isuzu Vehicross was a two-door compact SUV that never found a large audience. Fewer than 6,000 were built between its 1997 debut in Japan and the end of production in 2001.

The model reached U.S. showrooms shortly before 2000. By then, it had already been on sale in its home market. That left American buyers a short window to find one before production stopped.

The Vehicross used body-on-frame construction. In that layout, the body is mounted on a separate frame. Pickup trucks and dedicated off-road vehicles have long used the same approach. Most crossovers take the other route, with unibody construction that merges body and frame into a single structure.

The design also gave the Vehicross short overhangs. Little bodywork extends past the front and rear wheels. That lowers the chance of a bumper striking the ground at the base of a steep climb or descent. Off-roaders measure that clearance as the approach angle at the front and the departure angle at the rear.

A locking differential added another off-road tool. A conventional open differential lets each wheel on an axle turn at its own speed. On loose or uneven ground, the wheel with the least grip can spin while the other barely moves. When a locker is engaged, the wheels on an axle turn together. A wheel without grip cannot spin freely while the other one stays still.

The four-wheel-drive system worked toward the same goal. It relied on a dozen independent sensors to detect wheelspin. The system then shifted power to the wheels with the best traction. The locker and the sensor-driven system gave the Vehicross two separate ways to deal with a wheel that lost grip.

Those capabilities came with trade-offs. MotorTrend points to its high price and tight interior as weaknesses. Shoppers looking at a compact SUV had to accept both drawbacks to get the off-road hardware.

MotorTrend now counts the Vehicross among 10 cars that sold poorly yet have aged well.

The Mazdaspeed 6 is one of 10 cars that MotorTrend groups as cool cars that sold poorly when new but have since aged well.

The list covers models that struggled to find buyers in showrooms and later earned respect from enthusiasts. The Mazdaspeed 6 is the performance sedan on that list.

The Subaru SVX was a grand tourer styled by Giorgetto Giugiaro and powered by a 3.3-liter flat-six engine. It sold poorly, and Subaru built it for only one generation, from 1991 to 1996.

MotorTrend points out that the SVX cost a lot for a Subaru. The publication also judges that it was neither especially sporty nor especially practical.

Giugiaro, an Italian designer, gave the SVX a streamlined body with a spoiler. Its unusual side windows lower only partway.

MotorTrend calls that door glass a "window within a window" design. In MotorTrend's view, the glass and the car's futuristic bodywork now look bold.

The Santa Fe New Mexican describes the SVX as handling more softly than a BMW. The gap shows most in turns.

Early SVX models also had mechanical faults. Their transmissions were defective and could run too hot. Poor seals left the wheel bearings prone to failure.

The SVX appears on MotorTrend's list of 10 cool cars that sold poorly but later aged well.

The DMC DeLorean is the stainless-steel sports car that the 1985 film Back to the Future turned into a household name. Roughly 9,000 were built from 1981 to 1983, and then the company behind it collapsed.

The car took its name from John DeLorean, a former General Motors $GM designer. At GM, he helped set the template for the American muscle car with the first Pontiac GTO. His own company built the DeLorean in Northern Ireland, with production starting in 1981.

MotorTrend identifies three problems with the car itself. Its performance did not live up to the promise of its looks. Its build quality had serious flaws. Its price was high.

That price became a bigger obstacle because of when the car arrived. It reached showrooms during the early-1980s recession.

The car's fame came only after the business had failed. Back to the Future came out in 1985, two years after the company collapsed. By then, no new DeLoreans were being built. The film gave the car a public profile that its sales never did.

The DeLorean has since found a second life in the restomod scene. Restomod builders keep a classic car's original look while updating it with modern components. For the DeLorean, that interest gives the cars left from a short, troubled production run a new role among enthusiasts.

The BMW Z3 M Coupe was a niche M car whose sales were modest next to other BMW M models. A premium price and a shooting-brake-style two-seater body kept its audience small.

The car is nicknamed the Clown Shoe. BMW never planned to build it in high volume.

The body was the first hurdle. A shooting brake pairs a sports-car front end with a long roof and a squared-off tail. On a two-seat car, that layout looks closer to a small wagon than to a conventional coupe. The shape narrowed the pool of shoppers willing to buy one.

Price was the second hurdle. The M Coupe sat in premium territory. Shoppers ready to spend that much had a more conventional choice with the same badge, the BMW M3.

That choice meant the M Coupe competed with a model from its own lineup. It did not have to lose buyers to a rival brand to struggle. It could lose them to the M3 in the same showroom.

MotorTrend argues that time has turned the M Coupe's oddness into a virtue. In MotorTrend's view, the same shape that held back sales when the car was new now works in its favor.

The Dodge Magnum SRT-8 was a slow-selling station wagon powered by a 425-hp 6.1-liter V-8. Dodge built it from 2006 to 2008, when the entire Magnum line ended.

The Magnum shared its mechanical base with the Dodge Charger sedan. The SRT-8 version put the 6.1-liter V-8 from that performance lineup into a wagon body.

The result was a family-hauler shape on sedan running gear. Buyers who wanted the engine and the cargo space in one car had this option for three model years.

Dodge gave the 2008 models a refresh. That update lasted a single year. The Magnum line ended before the revised car could return for a second season.

MotorTrend reports that Dodge built fewer than 250 SRT-8s in 2008. On that count, the publication describes the 2008 cars as technically rarer than the Ferrari $RACE Enzo.

The comparison rests on build numbers alone. It does not weigh the two cars on price, performance or design.

The model year splits Magnum SRT-8s into two groups. The 2006 and 2007 cars predate the refresh. The 2008 cars carry the one-year update and the low production count that MotorTrend cites.

This slide falls short of the 300-word minimum. The source gives only a handful of facts on the Dodge Magnum SRT-8, and no approved supplementary details cover it.

The Acura NSX (NC1) is one of 10 cars on a MotorTrend list of models that sold poorly when new and have since earned respect from enthusiasts.

The Pontiac G8 GXP was a rear-drive sedan with a 415-hp V-8, built only for the 2009 model year. About 1,800 were made before General Motors $GM retired the Pontiac brand.

Beneath its Pontiac badges, the G8 GXP was a rebadged Holden Commodore SS. The Commodore SS came from Australia, where Holden sold it under its own name.

Power went to the rear wheels. Buyers could pair the V-8 with an optional six-speed manual transmission.

MotorTrend likened the car to an American BMW M5 in its first test of the G8 GXP. The comparison put a Pontiac sedan up against a performance benchmark from BMW's M division.

Timing worked against the car. The G8 GXP went on sale during the 2008 financial crisis, while General Motors, Pontiac's parent company, was struggling.

The model never reached a second year. Production ended with the 2009 model year, and the Pontiac name disappeared along with it.

MotorTrend reports that low-mileage G8 GXPs often sell at or above their original MSRP. Cars with the six-speed manual are the clearest examples of that pattern, according to the publication.

The Cadillac CTS-V Wagon put a 556-hp supercharged V-8 into a luxury wagon body, and fewer than 1,800 were built over a four-year run. MotorTrend calls it a future classic.

Cadillac developed the wagon's chassis tuning at the Nürburgring, the racing circuit in Germany. Buyers could pair the supercharged V-8 with an optional six-speed manual transmission.

A sleeper is a car whose ordinary appearance hides how fast it is. The CTS-V Wagon fit that description. From the outside, it looked like a premium family hauler with a cargo area at the back.

MotorTrend kept one of the wagons in its long-term test fleet. When that test ended in 2011, the magazine named the car one of the greatest sleeper hot rods.

Showroom demand never matched that reception. The low production total is what places the wagon on MotorTrend's list of sales flops.

The Volkswagen Phaeton sold poorly when it was new and has since gained respect, according to MotorTrend. The outlet includes it on a list of 10 cars that flopped in showrooms but have aged well.

5 scary driving scenarios (and what to do)

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

From tire blowouts to flooded roads, learn what to do in five frightening driving emergencies, and how to stay in control when the unexpected happens

Driving can feel routine until something unexpected happens. A deer darts across the road, a tire blows out or another driver suddenly heads straight toward you. In those moments, panic can take over, making it harder to react safely.

The good news is that knowing what to do before trouble starts can make a frightening situation easier to manage. You can't control every driver, weather event or hazard on the road, but you can prepare yourself to respond calmly and make decisions that reduce the risk of a crash.

Reader’s Digest shared advice for handling some of the most unsettling situations drivers can face, from losing control on a slippery road to encountering dangerous weather. The common thread is simple: stay as calm as possible, avoid sudden movements and prioritize getting yourself and others out of danger.

Here are five scary driving scenarios and what to do if they happen to you.

A deer appearing in the road can trigger an instinctive reaction to jerk the steering wheel out of its way. Resist that urge. Swerving can send your vehicle into another lane, into oncoming traffic or off the road.

Instead, keep both hands firmly on the wheel and apply the brakes firmly. Reader’s Digest recommends maintaining control of the vehicle rather than making a sudden evasive turn. If you need to steer, use a controlled shuffle-steering motion to keep both hands engaged.

It’s a frightening few seconds, especially when an animal appears without warning, but a predictable response is safer than a sharp maneuver. Focus on keeping your vehicle under control and avoid creating a second hazard while trying to avoid the first.

A blowout can make the car pull sharply and leave you feeling as though you've lost control. The instinct may be to slam on the brakes, but that can make the situation worse.

Reader’s Digest recommends staying calm, easing off the accelerator and gradually reducing speed. You can brake lightly as needed, then steer toward a safe place off the road. Keep a firm grip on the steering wheel, particularly if a front tire blows, since the vehicle may pull in an unexpected direction.

Avoid jerking the wheel or making abrupt corrections. Once you've reached a safe spot, get out of traffic's way before dealing with the tire or calling for help.

A blowout is unsettling, but the goal isn't to stop immediately. It's to slow down in a controlled way and get the vehicle somewhere safe.

Seeing a vehicle heading directly into your lane is one of the most terrifying things that can happen behind the wheel. You may have only seconds to react, so avoid freezing or staring at the oncoming car.

Reader’s Digest recommends trying to get the other driver's attention by honking and flashing your headlights while looking for a way to avoid a direct collision. If you need to move, focus your eyes on the safest available path, whether that's an open lane or the shoulder.

Your vehicle tends to follow your line of sight, so fixating on the approaching car can make it harder to steer away from danger. Keep your attention on where you want to go and make the safest controlled move available.

There's no guaranteed way to avoid a collision in this situation, but staying focused on an escape route gives you a better chance of responding deliberately instead of panicking.

A road covered in water can be deceptive. What looks like a shallow puddle may conceal a much deeper stretch, and moving water can sweep a vehicle away.

Reader’s Digest advises against driving through water more than four inches deep. Even relatively shallow flooding can damage your engine and the electronic systems in your vehicle. Never attempt to drive through moving water, since the current can carry your car away.

If you can't tell how deep the water is, don't assume it's safe. The safest choice is to find another route rather than risk crossing. If you determine that a stretch is shallow enough to cross, the article recommends letting oncoming traffic pass first and proceeding slowly and steadily.

The important thing is not to let impatience or pressure from traffic convince you to take a risk. Turning around may cost you a few minutes; driving into floodwater can put your life in danger.

Losing traction on a slippery road can make it feel as though your car has a mind of its own. The key is to avoid overcorrecting and focus on the direction you want the vehicle to travel.

Reader’s Digest explains that when the rear of a car slides, drivers should look toward the path they want to take rather than at the obstacle they're trying to avoid. Your steering tends to follow your eyes, so focusing on a safe route can help you guide the car back under control.

That doesn't mean making a sudden, dramatic turn. Keep your movements controlled and give yourself room to correct the skid without jerking the wheel.

Winter driving calls for extra patience, too. Slippery roads make it harder to slow down and stop, so leave more distance between your vehicle and the one ahead. If conditions are especially poor, slowing down or finding a safe place to stop is better than pushing on.

Blueair InvisibleMist review: A humidifier without the usual mess?

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

The Blueair InvisibleMist skips visible mist and white dust. Here's how it handles humidity, refills, cleaning, and everyday use

If you've ever run an ultrasonic humidifier, you probably know the cleanup routine: white dust on nearby furniture, damp spots around the machine, and another reminder to buy distilled water. The Blueair InvisibleMist takes a different approach. It uses evaporative technology to add moisture to the air without producing a visible cloud or scattering mineral dust around the room.

At $149.99, it's a relatively premium option for a compact humidifier. It has a 3.5-liter top-fill tank, automatic humidity control, app connectivity, and a washable tank. Blueair also advertises up to 48 hours of runtime, which sounds appealing if you'd rather not refill it every day.

The catch is that evaporative humidifiers work differently from mist-producing models, and independent testing found some limitations with runtime and cleaning. The InvisibleMist has plenty going for it, but whether it's worth the price depends on what you want from a humidifier.

The InvisibleMist is a compact evaporative humidifier designed for bedrooms, offices, and other medium-sized spaces. Its 3.5-liter tank and wick-based system work together to pull water through a filter and circulate air over it, adding moisture gradually rather than releasing droplets into the room.

That design is the main selling point. Minerals in tap water stay behind in the wick instead of becoming airborne, so you don't need to buy distilled water or wipe white residue off your furniture. The lack of visible mist also means fewer damp surfaces around the unit.

At 14.6 inches tall and 9.4 inches wide, the machine is small enough to fit on a dresser or side table. It includes three fan speeds, automatic humidity control, a built-in sensor, and a night mode. The Blueair app adds remote controls, scheduling, humidity settings, and filter-life tracking. The tank is dishwasher-safe, and the wick is designed to be replaced about every six months.

The InvisibleMist does a good job of bringing moisture into a dry room. Independent testing found that it could raise humidity from 30% to 60% in about 52 minutes, with a tested humidification capacity of 6 liters per day. That makes it a capable choice for a bedroom or medium-sized living space, even if it isn't built for very large rooms.

The evaporative system also has a natural limit: As the room gets more humid, the rate of evaporation slows. In one test, humidity rose quickly at first but leveled off a little above 50%. That's not necessarily a drawback. For most households, keeping humidity in a comfortable range is more useful than pushing it as high as possible.

Convenience is another strength. You can pour water directly into the top without removing the tank, and the automatic mode adjusts output based on the humidity sensor. The app is useful if you want to change settings from across the room or set a schedule. Night mode dims the display and keeps the fan running quietly.

The biggest drawback is the gap between the advertised runtime and what you may get in practice. Blueair promotes up to 48 hours on a fill, but independent testing found a much shorter runtime under its test conditions. Another evaluation measured just over 30 hours, while a separate lab test recorded 11 hours. Settings, airflow, and room conditions can make a significant difference, but don't assume you'll get two full days between refills.

Cleaning also takes more work than the dishwasher-safe tank might suggest. The tank is easy to access once removed, but getting to it means taking apart several pieces of the unit. Most components still need to be washed by hand, and the weekly cleaning routine can feel a little involved. The wick needs periodic replacement, too.

The controls are another minor frustration. The touchscreen is fairly dim and can be a little finicky, while the app offers useful basics rather than a particularly extensive set of smart features. And because this is an evaporative model, it won't deliver the same rapid humidity boost as some ultrasonic or warm-mist alternatives.

At $149.99, the InvisibleMist is also more expensive than many basic humidifiers. Its tap-water compatibility and low operating costs help justify the price over time, but shoppers who don't mind using distilled water may find cheaper options that offer longer runtimes.

The InvisibleMist makes the most sense for people who want a low-fuss way to manage dry indoor air without dealing with white dust or buying distilled water. Its compact size, quiet operation, automatic humidity control, and app features make it a good fit for bedrooms and home offices.

It's less appealing if you want a humidifier that can run for days without a refill or one that's especially easy to take apart and clean. People with very large rooms may also want a model with more capacity.

For everyday use in a medium-sized space, the Blueair offers a thoughtful alternative to traditional mist-producing humidifiers. Just go in knowing that the convenience of tap-water use comes with regular wick maintenance and more frequent refills than the advertised maximum runtime might suggest.

NextVision Announces Record Third Quarter 2026 Results

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Revenue Up 125% to $106.4m; Operating Margin Rises to 61.8%

RA'ANANA, Israel, Nov. 9, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- NextVision Stabilized Systems Ltd. (TASE: NXSN), a technology growth company that develops, manufactures and markets stabilized day- and night-vision imaging solutions for ground and aerial platforms such as micro and mini UAVs and drones, with industry-leading weight-to-size and performance ratios, today announced its financial results for the third quarter and first nine months of 2026.

Chen Golan, Chairman of NextVision, commented: "NextVision continues to strengthen its position in the global market, with significant revenue growth and improved profitability. One of our most important growth engines is the expansion of activity with a growing customer base. Alongside our largest customers, we see additional customers increasing the scale of their activity with us and becoming more significant for the Company. This trend reflects confidence in our technology and the opportunities ahead of us for continued growth.

"We are already preparing for the activity levels of the coming years, expanding our production capacity, improving efficiency and continuing to invest in infrastructure, supported by the Company's strong cash flow. As part of this strategy, we are building a global manufacturing network, with our European production line establishing its operations and plans to expand production to the United States as well. Our presence in key markets will allow us to be closer to our customers, address local-manufacturing requirements and strengthen our ability to compete for additional business opportunities.

"Looking ahead, we are working to deepen NextVision's position as a technology partner of the world's leading platform manufacturers, and to expand the integration of our systems into additional platforms and missions. Alongside continued independent development and organic growth, targeted acquisitions of complementary technologies and capabilities are part of our strategy to broaden our solutions portfolio, strengthen our competitive advantage and build the Company's next growth engines. We see a market with significant potential, and we continue to invest in order to increase NextVision's place in it for years to come."

Revenue in the third quarter grew 125% to $106.4 million, compared with $47.3 million in the same quarter last year. Revenue for the first nine months of the year totaled $261.9 million, an increase of 117%.

The Company grew in all geographic regions. Europe continues to be its largest market, with revenue of $164.6 million in the first nine months, an increase of 138%. Revenue from North America rose 80% to $57.1 million, alongside growth in Israel and in additional markets.

The Company has more than 300 customers worldwide, of which 185 were active during the first nine months of 2026. Revenue from customers beyond the three largest customers totaled approximately $105 million, of which approximately $52 million was in the third quarter alone.

Gross profit totaled $71.0 million, an increase of 118% compared with $32.5 million in the same quarter last year. Gross margin in the third quarter improved to 66.7%, compared with 65.2% in the second quarter of the year. Efficiencies in manufacturing processes and higher productivity per employee support higher levels of activity and the maintenance of high profitability, even against the backdrop of rising raw material and component costs.

Operating profit jumped 133% to $65.8 million, compared with $28.3 million in the same quarter last year. The operating margin improved in the third quarter to 61.8%, after 56.9% in the first quarter and 58.8% in the second quarter, and compared with 59.8% in the same quarter last year. While revenue more than doubled, operating expenses grew at a significantly more moderate rate and their share of revenue declined to 4.9%, compared with 8.9% in the same quarter last year. These figures illustrate the Company's ability to translate revenue growth into improved profitability.

Net profit grew 113% to $60.1 million in the third quarter, compared with $28.2 million in the same quarter last year. Net profit for the first nine months totaled $152 million, more than double the $72 million in the same period last year.

Cash flow from operating activities in the first nine months totaled $128.4 million, 3.6 times the $35.2 million in the same period last year, after investing approximately $38.9 million in an inventory increase. In the third quarter alone, operating cash flow reached $53.3 million, almost three times the $18.8 million in the same quarter last year, after an inventory increase of approximately $20.8 million. The cash flow reflects the Company's ability to generate cash while, at the same time, stocking up on components to support the expansion of production and shorter delivery times.

Order backlog as of the date of this release totaled $209.3 million, compared with $124 million at the same time last year, an increase of 69%, and compared with $265.2 million at the time of the second-quarter results. The decrease compared with the previous quarter was affected, among other things, by the increase in delivery volumes, alongside the timing of new orders that are expected to be received over the coming weeks. Streamlined work processes and training contribute to higher productivity per employee and increased output, enabling the Company to convert orders into revenue faster. This capability strengthens the Company's competitive advantage in responding quickly to customer needs and in aligning production with their growth.

Production Line in Europe: The Company is advancing the establishment of its production capability in Europe. The move is part of the Company's strategic plan to expand its global manufacturing infrastructure, and is intended to strengthen the Company's presence in the European market, address customers who prefer European manufacturing and broaden the business opportunities available to the Company in the region. The Company is in the process of establishing a European subsidiary to operate the production line.

Every virtual megawatt needs a meter

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Last month, Google announced a deal with Voltus for a virtual power plant (VPP) of up to 1,070 MW in PJM, roughly the output of a large nuclear reactor. Rather than build a plant, Voltus will offer homes and businesses financial incentives to trim usage when the grid is strained, through smart thermostats, batteries and flexible equipment.

The deal represents an inflection point for the power and utilities sector, where “speed to power” innovations are defining the moment. It also underscores the importance of the equipment that will decide how far such programs go: the electric meter.

NEMA's 2026 Grid Reliability Study projects U.S. economy-wide electricity demand as much as 55% by 2050, with data center consumption alone growing 300% over the next decade. New generation and transmission can't keep pace, as permitting, capital needs and equipment and skilled labor backlogs protracting project delivery timelines.

As a result, operators are looking for capacity in the system they already have. On the transmission side, grid-enhancing technologies show what's possible: PJM modeling finds they could unlock between six and seven GW of usable capacity.

At the grid edge, the opportunity may be larger. Demand response contributed 33.3 GW of enrolled wholesale capacity in 2024, about 6.5% of peak demand in organized markets and could reach 20% of U.S. peak by 2030. Behind-the-meter resources, which include microgrids and aggregations of customer-sited resources (i.e., VPPs), are scaling too.

What these grid-edge resources share is a need for measurement. A demand response event or VPP dispatch only has value if someone can prove the load dropped and pay the customer who dropped it. That job falls to the electric meter.

Despite the momentum, only 6.5% of U.S. electricity customers are enrolled in retail demand response programs. NEMA's research points to several reasons: metering and communication rules written for large power plants that burden small participants, too little public data on how flexible resources perform during grid emergencies and market rules that vary by region.

Underneath them is a question of trust. Some grid operators and utilities still doubt that distributed resources will deliver when called. But confidence at scale requires consistent, verifiable measurement across millions of devices and many vendors.

That is the role of ANSI NEMA C12.1-2026. C12.1 sets performance criteria for new types of watthour and demand meters, defines accuracy classes and test methods and establishes acceptable in-service performance for revenue meters. When a meter reading decides whether a customer is paid for curtailment, or whether an operator can count an aggregation as capacity, those criteria make the number credible.

Timing makes this pressing. Millions of first-generation smart meters, many installed between 2010 and 2015 with American Recovery and Reinvestment Act funding, are reaching end of life. Utilities in every region are planning or executing large-scale replacements through the late 2020s, driven by aging hardware, obsolete communications and the need to support distributed energy resources, EVs and resilience. Indeed, as NEMA member company Honeywell has noted, first-wave meters lack the speed and accuracy of newer models and networks and security practices evolve faster than meters can be replaced. This is a replacement market, not a first deployment and the meters chosen now will likely stay in service for 15 to 20 years or more.

The 2026 edition of C12.1 arrives at the right moment. It folds the former C12.20 accuracy-class standard into a single document covering the entire metering code and adds guidance on non-Blondel metering. Referencing C12.1-2026 in procurement specifications gives utilities, regulators and vendors a common baseline for safety, accuracy and interoperability across vendors and networks and protects ratepayers' two-decade investment.

The same long-term lens applies to where grid equipment comes from. NEMA's Make It American® program is an industry-led, third party-certification program. Accredited auditors evaluate manufacturers against the NEMA 70901-2026 process standard and certified facilities and products are listed in a public Build America, Buy America registry. The Federal Highway Administration and the Texas, Arkansas and Montana transportation departments recognize it and a pending bill in the U.S. Senate features language treating Make It American documentation as sufficient evidence of domestic-content compliance.

NEMA has prioritized product-level specifications for the equipment utilities buy most, including high- and medium-voltage distribution equipment, grid management and automation equipment and wire and cable. That lets procurement teams verify domestic content product-by-product, with certification that reflects actual production practices.

Virtual power plants may be the fastest new capacity on the market. But a virtual megawatt is only as real as the meter that proves it was delivered.

Congress is coming back to Washington. Here's what to expect

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Lawmakers are returning to Washington this week to finish up work before Democrats take control of the House and Senate in January. Zayrha Rodriguez/NPR hide caption

It is the final hoorah for the 119th Congress. Republicans have just weeks left in control of the House of Republicans and the Senate. The main thing on the agenda: keeping the federal government open. Funding is set to run out on December 11, when a stop-gap measure passed about a month ahead of the midterms expires.

Any funding extension is all but certain to be done through another short-term bill, known as a continuing resolution. The typical government funding process — 12 carefully negotiated appropriations bills — would take time and political will that lawmakers currently lack.

Congress will also need to find a path forward on the National Defense Authorization Act, long considered a must-pass annual military funding package. This year, the bill is quagmired in the unforgiving politics of the Iran war. The conflict is opposed by a majority of Americans — something President Trump has acknowledged was a key force in driving his party out of power.

The House is also likely to vote on an impeachment effort targeting Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth. Introduced by Republican Thomas Massie of Kentucky, a key figure in forcing the administration's release of the Epstein files, the impeachment measure lists a number of charges, including that the administration failed to properly consult Congress on the military campaign.

Democrats are unlikely to be in a compromising mood on legislation, just weeks away from taking control of both chambers. That means continued work on big-ticket items like the Protect College Sports Act, meant to introduce new regulations around college athlete compensation, and a bipartisan energy permitting overhaul will likely wait until the new term.

With slim majorities in both chambers, they could also have to contend with some attendance problems after a demoralizing performance in last week's midterm elections. That further complicates passing legislation through chambers already governed by the thinnest of majorities.

Mike Ricci, a Republican strategist with the group Seven Letter and aide to former Speakers of the House John Boehner and Paul Ryan, told NPR that a number of forces could help draw reluctant lame duck legislators back to Washington, including winding down their offices, finding new work and attending the White House Christmas party.

How did education issues fare on state ballots in 2026?

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

From early childhood education initiatives to curbing school choice, the blue wave is expected to trigger a range of policy changes in K-12.

Democrats scored a historic win in the 2026 midterm elections — and, as part of the blue wave that gave Democrats control of Congress, voters also flipped governors' seats in Republican stronghold states and cast their ballots on key education issues.

The flip of Congress beginning in 2027 has already had an impact at the federal level, with Rep. Bobby Scott, D-Va., sending letters to heads of federal agencies, including to U.S. Secretary of Education Linda McMahon, warning them to preserve records and other information or risk facing "criminal penalties."

On the state level, voters flipped governors from Republican to Democrat in Georgia, Iowa, Nevada, Ohio and Texas, with potential implications for state education leadership and district takeovers.

Democrats also took control of several state legislative chambers — impacting education issues in those states — such as in Georgia, Minnesota, Michigan, North Carolina, Texas and Wisconsin.

Additionally, voters cast their ballots on issues related to school sports, early childhood issues, and public funding.

"Across the nation, most Americans want the same thing — a more affordable future, strong public schools and safer communities," said National Education Association President Princess Moss in a Nov. 3 statement. "Tonight, voters came together across race, place and gender and sent a clear message and mandate: put working families, students and educators first and deliver for our communities, not billionaires and corporations."

Here are key takeaways from the state races:

Although many state legislatures have set school policies related to transgender youth in recent years, those issues were on a handful of state ballots on election night.

In Arizona, voters approved Proposition 318, which requires schools and athletic associations to restrict sports participation and access to intimate facilities based on sex, preventing transgender students’ access to those programs and spaces.

A similar measure in Nebraska, Initiative 442, also overwhelmingly passed, as well as in Washington.

Early childhood education issues were front and center for many of the gubernatorial races, according to a New America analysis.

In Georgia, Democratic Gov.-elect Keisha Lance Bottoms has pushed for mandatory kindergarten and universal preschool for 4-year-olds, per New America, a nonpartisan public policy think tank.

Democrat David Crowley, who is set to become Wisconsin's first Black governor, has proposed a plan for universal early childhood education for children ages 5 and younger.

And in New Mexico, Democrat Gov.-elect Deb Haaland has advocated to expand the universal child care already established under her Democratic predecessor Michelle Lujan Grisham.

The expansion of private school choice programs has been a major highlight for the Republican party in recent years, and the election of more Democrats likely means increased oversight of or changes to those programs. Opponents say private school choice programs, paid for through taxpayer funds, fleece funds from public school systems and lack transparency for student outcomes, while supporters say they give families opportunities to pick schools that best serve their child's needs.

“In race after race, those who supported public schools and rejected private school vouchers triumphed — statewide in Texas, Iowa, Michigan and Ohio, and in countless congressional districts and school board races," said Randi Weingarten, president of the American Federation of Teachers, in a Nov. 3 statement.

Across the nation, Democratic candidates made strides in the 2026 election cycle by promising to bolster public education spending.

In Texas for example, Gov.-elect Gina Hinojosa, a former Austin Independent School District board member, nodded to the issue, saying she "believes in Texas public schools" and saw her "child's school under threat."

“The days of treating our public schools like they are for sale are over,” she said in her victory speech Tuesday night.

In Minnesota, voters approved a constitutional amendment to increase funding going to school districts without raising individual income or property taxes starting July 1, 2027. It will raise the contribution to school districts by approximately $33 per student. If the measure were in place for 2025, the Minneapolis Public School District, for example, would have received about $3 million in funding, rather than the $2 million it was appropriated.

Colorado’s Poudre School District is closing six schools and consolidating two others after the district reports it has about 10,500 open seats.

Liverpool: Trey Nyoni impact under Andoni Iraola cannot be ignored

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

By Axel Clody | 09 Nov 2026 09:57

In a transfer market that moves as many millions as the Premier League’s, where all 20 clubs are looking to invest in signings that can raise the level of their squads, the answer is often already within the club. One such case is Liverpool’s.

Trey Nyoni’s performances so far, a midfielder of just 19, show that the youngster could be the solution for the starting XI until the end of the season. With maturity above the average, the Englishman shows excellent vision, courage on the ball and quality in building play.

Against Arsenal earlier this month, in the Premier League, he led the match in accurate passes (53), pass accuracy (96%) and line-breaking passes (14), according to Opta data.

Against Fenerbahce last Wednesday, he then scored his first goal for Liverpool in a secure performance in midfield. The numbers with and without the ball also caught the eye.

Accurate passes: 89/96 (93%) Passes in the opposition half: 58/64 (91%) Passes in own half: 31/32 (97%) Long balls: 4/5 (80%) Tackles: 2/2 Interceptions: 3 Ball recoveries: 9 Ground duels: 2/4 Blocked shots: 1

In a midfield that is falling short, Nyoni is the positive news that could change Liverpool’s outlook.

That was Nyoni’s 12th game for Liverpool at the start of this season, half of them as a starter. The youngster was discovered in 2023, when he stood out for Leicester City’s Under-18s at just 15 years old.

His prominence in Liverpool’s youth system soon caught the attention of then manager Jurgen Klopp. At 16, he made his debut for Liverpool at Anfield, in an FA Cup match. Before the youngster replaced Harvey Elliott, Klopp and Nyoni looked at each other and smiled together, perhaps both already knowing the future in store for the gem.

In the last transfer window, Liverpool turned down a £30m proposal from Nottingham Forest because they believe in the talent and future of their prospect. So far, that faith looks justified.

The club and the player are in advanced talks over a new contract. The board understand that the midfielder, like winger Rio Ngumoha, is one of two gems with an enormous future who can put their names in the history of the club.

The good news for Nyoni is also that the opportunities he has received from Iraola can be seen more as a belief that he will be a starter for a long time than as a rotation strategy, merely offering a few minutes to a young reserve.

At Bournemouth, Iraola led a talented team of players under 23, with names such as Rayan and Eli Junior Kroupi, among others. He not only managed them, but developed the prospects, raising the talent of each one.

In a season in which Liverpool have many question marks and few answers, Nyoni looks a success. There is still plenty of room for improvement for the gem, but the future, and the present itself, are promising.

Aston Villa have reached a verbal agreement for left-back Ian Maatsen to sign a new contract at the club, sources have told Football Insider.

Emery remains firmly committed to the project he is building, despite links with elite jobs elsewhere in recent weeks.

What Are The Best Seats To Book On A Widebody Long-Haul Flight?

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

There is no single answer for where you should try to sit on a long-haul widebody flight because it all depends on what sort of traveler you are and what you want to prioritize. The best seat depends on whether you care most about sleep, legroom, getting off quickly, or relaxing. Whatever you prioritize, you'll have to trade off other aspects, such as convenient access to the toilet or the best window views.

Undoubtedly, the best place to sit in a widebody flight is in first or business class. Economy class varies a lot depending on where you sit, so this article focuses on that.

The first step in choosing the right seat for your economy class widebody flight is understanding how the cabin works. This is because an economy class cabin is so large on a widebody aircraft that it acts more like a set of mini-cabins, which are broken up by exit rows, galleys, toilets, and more. Some superjumbos also have an economy class enclave on the upper deck. Each mini-section of the economy class cabin is suited to different passenger archetypes.

The table below shows what you can expect in each part of the economy class cabin, making use of insights from Seat Compare:

Less busy, rapid service, helps with sleeping, and you can get off quickly

Proximity to the galley and curtain behind the premium classes

Lots of choice; often you don't have to pay too much extra for seat selection

The most activity and busiest part of the cabin will make sleeping difficult

Proximity to the galley and toilets. You may get meal service last

Rarely found due to the commonality of putting premium classes on the upper deck, and the small number of double-decker jets still flying.

Additional room, better views out of the windows, small economy class cabin

Stairs that can lead to separate boarding, and it takes longer to get off the aircraft.

In modern aviation, most airlines now expect you to pay extra to choose a specific seat. Some of the most in-demand seats, such as the exit row, cost more, though still well below premium economy or business class. As a result, an additional option opens up, particularly for solo travelers. That is to refuse to pay for seat selection and gamble that you won't get put right next to the toilet.

The most well-known path to extra legroom in economy class is to book a seat in the exit row. The extra few inches of legroom makes the seat feel closer to premium economy. It is ideal for taller passengers and often means you won't need to get up to let fellow passengers out.

Please note that these seats come with responsibility in the event of an accident. Each airline has a different minimum age to sit here, usually between 15 and 18 years old, and you also need to be physically able to operate the emergency exit.

This isn't the only reason that the exit row can be a poisoned chalice. First, aviation rules mean you can't keep any bags under the seat in front of you, as these could cause an obstruction if the aircraft must be evacuated. You'll need to be organized if you want a specific device, book, or magazine during takeoff and landing. Exit-row seats can also frustrate some passengers. These seats are fixed, often with no recline and armrests that can't be moved. The armrests also make the seat a little narrower than normal. Robustly built passengers may find this cramped. The tray tables are built into the armrest rather than on the back of the seat, which can make for an odd eating experience. For some passengers, these downsides will be irrelevant, but for others, they make exit row seats hellish.

Finding the best seat in the house can be tricky.

A poor night's sleep on a red-eye long-haul flight can steal a day from your hard-earned vacation time, making it a priority for many economy class passengers. Yet, given the inherent discomfort and crowding of economy class cabins, this can be impossible. Some seats within the class will still give you the best chance of sleep.

The first step is to secure a window seat. You'll have a wall to lean on or wedge a pillow or sweater against for additional comfort. It also means you won't be woken up when the other passengers in your row want to use the bathroom. The window seat allows you to control the blinds, though an eye mask is always a good idea.

The table below uses data collected by Simple Flying to show the window dimensions of various widebody aircraft. Pick a flight with a large window if you want to get the best views:

10.7 inches x 18.4 inches (27.2 cm x 46.7 cm)

9.5 inches x 13.5 inches (24.1 cm x 34.3 cm)

10 inches x 15 inches (25.4 cm x 38.1 cm)

9 inches x 12.3 inches (22.9 cm x 31.2 cm)

The best spots are toward the front of the economy class cabin, in front of the wings due to the engine noise, although not far forward enough to be disturbed by cabin crew clattering around in the galley. This spot, near the plane's center of gravity, also helps you avoid turbulence that can jolt you awake. Another great spot is a mini-cabin, a smaller economy class enclave formed by galleys, exit rows, and other objects that divide the cabin. These mini-cabins have fewer people, so they have less activity that could disturb your sleep.

Another popular seat that is available throughout the aircraft is the aisle seat. It has numerous benefits. Most obviously, it is more spacious than the average economy class seat, as you can enjoy some of the room in the aisle when passengers and cabin crew aren't coming past. That gives the impression of more width than the basic amount that the seat offers.

You'll find deplaning much easier, as you won't have to wait for the passenger next to you to finish before you can move. That makes the seat ideal if you have a connecting flight or need to get to your accommodation quickly. The seat also lets you access the bathroom easily, although you will also have to move whenever the other passengers in your row need to get up. Likewise, you can easily access the overhead bins, but will be disturbed when the passengers next to you need their items.

Yet, the aisle seat usually isn't ideal for passengers who want to be undisturbed or enjoy a good night's sleep. Due to the size of long-haul economy class cabins, there will constantly be other passengers and flight attendants traveling down the aisle. They could bump into you and wake you from your slumber.

Which seat should you choose for your next long-haul flight?

No seat combines everything you want to avoid on long-haul widebody flights like the final row of economy class. As you board, you may struggle to find space for your bags as the overhead bins may be partially filled with airline emergency equipment. During the flight, you'll often have no or limited recline because the seat is directly against a wall. You'll also often be close to noises and smells from the lavatory and galley. If there is a queue for the lavatory, it will always be directly next to you.

It is also common for meal services to work from the front to the back of the aircraft. Along with the wait, the best options may already be taken by the time the trolley gets to you. Finally, the rear of the aircraft is set back from its center of gravity, so you'll get the worst turbulence. The problems continue when it's time to get off the aircraft. You will often be one of the last people off the aircraft. That can cause problems if you have a tight connection. You also won't be able to get ahead of the rush to deal with immigration controls, which can cause even more delays.

The middle seat anywhere on the aircraft is also a must-avoid. You'll feel cramped between two other passengers and have no window view. This seat can become a disaster if the passengers on either side of you are large individuals. As with the window seat, you'll have to disturb the passenger next to you whenever you want to get up.

Found an error? Send it info@simpleflying.com so it can be corrected.

Lululemon Athletica vs. On: Which Consumer Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Lululemon's steady profits stand in contrast to On's blistering revenue growth, but its declining sales raise a key question.

Investors face a choice between established market leadership and high-growth momentum when comparing Lululemon Athletica (LULU -0.88%) and On (ONON -0.48%). Both companies are vying for dominance in the competitive athletic category.

Lululemon is known for its high-end yoga and technical apparel, whereas On has built its reputation on innovative running footwear. This comparison explores their financial health and market positioning to determine the better long-term pick.

Lululemon Athletica designs and sells technical athletic apparel, footwear, and accessories through an omni-channel model. It reaches customers via more than 800 company-operated stores and a robust e-commerce platform across multiple countries.

The company also leverages wholesale channels by partnering with fitness studios and university retailers. This diversified approach helps it maintain high brand visibility and reach a loyal customer base in the apparel sector.

In FY 2025, revenue reached nearly $11.1 billion, representing revenue growth of approximately 4.9% compared to the prior year. Net income for the period was roughly $1.6 billion.

On develops and distributes premium performance sports products, primarily focusing on its innovative footwear technology. As of the end of 2025, the company operated nearly 70 retail locations worldwide and maintains a strong presence through wholesale partners and its direct-to-consumer e-commerce platform.

Its growth strategy emphasizes global expansion, with a significant retail footprint already established in China. The company relies on its high-performance brand image to attract runners and lifestyle consumers willing to pay a premium price.

In FY 2025, revenue reached roughly $3.7 billion, which reflects impressive revenue growth. Net income for the fiscal period was approximately $246 million.

Lululemon Athletica faces several legal and operational hurdles, including multiple class action lawsuits regarding its financial guidance and brand health. The company is also navigating a management transition at the CEO position. External factors like changes in U.S. tariff policy and supply chain concentration in Asia add further uncertainty. Increased competition from rivals that can quickly imitate technical designs may also pressure its historical brand premium.

On must navigate the challenges of scaling a global logistics network while competing against much larger footwear giants like Nike. Maintaining its premium pricing and brand exclusivity becomes more difficult as the company moves into broader wholesale distribution. Inventory management is a critical risk, as any miscalculation in demand could lead to heavy discounting that hurts net margin. The company also remains sensitive to shifts in consumer discretionary spending across its primary markets in Europe and North America.

Lululemon currently offers a more conservative valuation profile, while On carries a premium multiple that reflects its significantly faster revenue growth trajectory.

Each company provides slightly different offerings, but both are generally in the fitness and activewear segment. Both are considered premium brands and can struggle when consumers tighten their budgets. They may like Lululemon pants or shoes from On, but in tighter times, a consumer may just trade down to a different brand with similar items at lower prices. That said, there's still opportunity to find the right stocks in that sector of the market. According to Fortune Business Insights, the global sportswear market is expected to grow from $235 billion in 2026 to $402.5 billion by 2034.

For this battle, I would choose On for one main reason. The reason is that On is more in the opening stretch of its journey, with less than 100 stores. In comparison, Lululemon ended 2025 with 811 stores, and it's transitioning into a more mature company, looking for what its next stage will look like. Also, you can see it in the sales growth. For the second quarter of 2026, On reported that revenue increased 13.5%, while Lululemon reported net revenue decreased by 4%. Ultimately, anyone interested in On should dig into its financials a bit more before considering it as an investment. Still, based solely on a comparison with Lululemon, I'd rather own On at this moment in time.

Jack Delaney has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Nike and On Holding. The Motley Fool recommends Lululemon Athletica Inc. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Donald Trump’s ’60 Minutes’ Interview Was A Mixture Of Denial, Reflection And Contradiction Following Midterm Drubbing

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

In his first sit down interview since his midterm drubbing, Donald Trump insisted on 60 Minutes that candidates would have done better had he been on the ballot.

He blamed voters’ dismal views of the economy on “bad messaging.”

And he ran through a number of explanations for why he made $3 billion since the start of his second term, feeding into the corruption storyline that proved to be so important at the ballot box.

Correspondent Ross Douthat, though, also got the president admitting that his deportation policy “might be too tough.” Related Stories Late Night TV James Talarico Stuns 'SNL' With Post-Midterms Cameo & "Trump Freezers" Swipe News UFC's Dana White As White House Press Secretary? Trump Says "Please Take The Job!"

Since the election wipeout, Trump has seemingly veered from being at his own kind of peace with the results and striking notes of defiance. He congratulated Democrats on election night, but also has reposted some election conspiracy theories. Watch on Deadline

Just an hour after the 60 Minutes sit down on Friday morning, in which he talked about working with Democrats on immigration reform, he then went ahead named Stephen Miller, the architect of his deportation crackdown, as his chief of staff, replacing Susie Wiles.

Douthat skillfully questioned Trump over a number of topics, interrupting at points and following up at others, and getting to some places that other interviews did not. Since the midterm losses, Trump has spoken to reporters and did a phone interview with CNN, but 60 Minutes showed that there is still nothing like a sit down interview.

That was particularly the case as Douthat questioned Trump on corruption, in particular with Trump’s stake in World Liberty Financial.

“So your son manages or helps manage a cryptocurrency company called World Liberty Financial….You are co-owner of that company.”

Douthat replied, “This company is also partially owned by investors connected to the government of the United Arab Emirates. I think if I told you that story and it was about the Biden family, if I said Joe Biden is the part owner of a company and it has all of these investments from the Middle East, you would say, ‘That sounds corrupt,’ right.”

Douthat then added, “Why is this different?”

Trump said, “I’m gonna tell you why. Because I know nothing about it.”

But Douthat noted that Trump “could just walk away from that and then you would not be accused of corruption.”

Trump replied, “My sons run the company. It’s hard to say to them, ‘Don’t do anything anymore for four years because I’m president.'”

Douthat kept pressing him on whether he thought that foreign government investors in World Liberty were trying to buy influence.

Trump’s reply ultimately was that every president “has a problem,” even citing George Washington. In fact, in the extended version of the interview, Trump cited Barack Obama’s deal with Netflix and that he “got $100 million to make a movie.”

“What Obama did after his presidency was different,” Douthat said, while Trump claimed otherwise.

When sizing up the midterms, Trump acknowledged that the war in Iran had led to higher gas prices and that there’s “a great dislike, incredibly, for Israel.” But he showed little sign of accepting much of any blame, insisting that “if I was on the ballot, it would’ve been a much different situation because you saw the level of popularity and love… We had massive rallies.” At many of those rallies, he asked voters to pretend he was on the ballot.

He also blamed the press, complaining that when he comes to things like jobs numbers, “the press doesn’t cover positive news for Republicans.” (For the record, Deadline, along with many other outlets, covered Friday’s most recent labor report).

Douthat said, “You and the media, you and our business have been in tension throughout your career. But you still won elections, right?…So why couldn’t you win this time.”

“I think it was bad messaging,” Trump said.

The final questions of the interview had to do with whether Trump thought he would get to heaven and whether he could forgive his enemies.

“The answer is no. I want to be honest, no,” Trump said.

“Before the judgment throne of God?” Douthat said.

Trump said, “Before the judgment. No, I think they were very bad people and … I can use the word hate, I do hate some of my enemies.”

Douthat replied, “Mr. President. You have had the most charmed and remarkable life of any American in my entire lifetime, and you are telling me that before the judgment throne of God you couldn’t find it in your heart to forgive some of your enemies? After all that’s been given to you? Come on. Help me out here.”

Trump said he could not. “I probably cannot do that, no, because I do hate some of my enemies and I always will.”

By bringing patients, providers, and insurers together, we aim to remove barriers to care and help more people find the right support for their needs.

*Estimated effective hourly earnings are for licensed Psychiatric Mental Health Nurse Practitioners in Wyoming and are illustrative estimates derived from modeled assumptions, not a guaranteed hourly rate, wage, or salary. These estimates are calculated using assumptions regarding number of completed visits per hour, CPT code mix, and utilization of add-on codes (such as 90833) when clinically appropriate and properly documented. Actual earnings vary and are dependent on individual clinical decisions, case mix, scheduling availability, payer reimbursement, licensure, and geographic market factors.

Colts are expected to take best offer for Jonathan Taylor at deadline

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The trade chatter regarding Colts running back Jonathan Taylor has lasted for two weeks. It will last at least two more days.

The current belief in league circles is that the Colts will take the best offer that’s on the table as the deadline approaches at 4:00 p.m. ET on Tuesday.

Sure, the Colts would like to get as much as they can, ideally landing the same package the Panthers got from the 49ers for running back Christian McCaffrey. But the Colts are likely to take what they can get, with the market for Taylor set by the competition among the teams that make an offer.

Regardless, the 2-7 Colts are ready to sell. At this point, they should be ready to move anyone — and everyone. They’re hoping to stockpile draft picks for the next General Manager. The more picks that compile, the more attractive the job becomes.

So whether it’s the teams that have been linked to Taylor (Giants, Vikings, Seahawks, Broncos, Packers) or someone unexpected (like the 49ers), a trade is expected to happen before the clock strikes 4:00 p.m. ET on Tuesday, November 10.
